{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_0134AHi4aZHScN3bFAmrYu1W","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→Behavior and video tracking\n     2→\n     3→Mice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\n     4→quency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\n     5→response trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\n     6→\n     7→Two CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\n     8→\n     9→Multi-regional electrophysiological recordings\n    10→\n    11→We designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\n    12→\n    13→Extracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\n    14→\n    15→Probes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\n    16→\n    17→Photoinhibition\n    18→\n    19→Light from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\n    20→\n    21→To analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\n    22→time window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\n    23→\n    24→Spike sorting and quality control\n    25→\n    26→We developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\n    27→\n    28→The details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2. \n    29→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"811f889a-a17c-4d8e-bdca-d7f0c4369b52","uuid":"a0f1bf36-e613-4991-865e-0201145548df","timestamp":"2026-07-29T03:36:19.074Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/app/methods.txt","content":"Behavior and video tracking\n\nMice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\nquency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\nresponse trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\n\nTwo CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\n\nMulti-regional electrophysiological recordings\n\nWe designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\n\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\n\nProbes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\n\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\ntime window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\n\nSpike sorting and quality control\n\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\n\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). Usually it's fine to read and directly store it in Python.\n    26→\n    27→Comment: in case 2, when using isinstance(data, data_type), if data could be a number, consider the possibility of data being either float or int, i.e. use\n    28→    if isinstance(data, float) or isinstance(data, int):\n    29→        do something\n    30→\"\"\"\n    31→\n    32→plt.rcParams['pdf.fonttype'] = 42\n    33→plt.rcParams['ps.fonttype'] = 42\n    34→\n    35→\n    36→def sliding_histogram(spikeTimes, begin_time, end_time, bin_width, stride, rate=True):\n    37→    '''\n    38→    Calculates the number of spikes for each unit in each sliding bin of width bin_width, strided by stride.\n    39→    begin_time and end_time are treated as the lower- and upper- bounds for bin centers.\n    40→    if rate=True, calculate firing rates instead of bin spikes.\n    41→    '''\n    42→    bin_begin_time = begin_time\n    43→    bin_end_time = end_time\n    44→    # This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    45→    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n    46→        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    47→    else:\n    48→        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    49→    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n    50→\n    51→    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n    52→\n    53→    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    54→    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n    55→        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n    56→\n    57→    if rate:\n    58→        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\n    59→    return binCenters, binSpikes\n    60→\n    61→\n    62→def process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0, end_time=3.5, save_folder=os.path.join(os.pardir, 'data_processed'), qc_mode='classifier'):\n    63→    \"\"\"\n    64→    Process all probe files in one session.\n    65→\n    66→    Args:\n    67→        data_folder (str): the folder containing all data including QC neuron ID files.\n    68→        file_path_list: list of full file paths of all probes in this session.\n    69→        bw (float): in sec.\n    70→        stride (float): in sec.\n    71→        qc_mode (str): 'classifier' or 'old'.\n    72→    \"\"\"\n    73→    print(qc_mode)\n    74→    if qc_mode == 'classifier':\n    75→        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'goodunits')\n    76→        print(qc_folder)\n    77→    elif qc_mode == 'old':\n    78→        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'DJ_GoodUnitsIdx_14regions_old_qc')\n    79→    else:\n    80→        raise ValueError('Wrong qc_mode! Must be \"classifier\" or \"old\".')\n    81→    \n    82→    assert os.path.exists(qc_folder)\n    83→\n    84→    spike_times_sess = []\n    85→    unit_info_sess = []\n    86→    unit_qc_sess_list = []\n    87→    ccf_coordinate_sess = []\n    88→    ccf_label_sess = []\n    89→    ccf_unit_id_sess = []\n    90→\n    91→    # ----- 1. concatenate all neurons from all probes -----\n    92→    for i_file, file_path in enumerate(sorted(file_path_list)):\n    93→        filename = os.path.split(file_path)[-1]\n    94→        name_list = filename[:-4].split('_') # ['map-export, 'SC038', '20191119', '115109', 's4', 'p1']\n    95→        mouse = name_list[1] # 'SC038'\n    96→        date = name_list[2] # '20191119'\n    97→        number = name_list[3]\n    98→        session = int(name_list[4][1:]) # 4\n    99→        probe = int(name_list[5][1:]) # 1\n   100→        sess_name = '%s_%s_%d' % (mouse, date, session)\n   101→        sess_name_qc = '_'.join([mouse, date, number]) # 'SC038_20191119_115109'\n   102→\n   103→        save_folder_sess = os.path.join(save_folder, 'stride%d_bw%d'%(stride*1000, bw*1000), sess_name)\n   104→        if not os.path.exists(save_folder_sess):\n   105→            os.makedirs(save_folder_sess)\n   106→\n   107→        if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'others_finished.txt')):\n   108→            print('%s has been processed. Each element is a list of 0 (lick left) and 1 (lick right)\n   122→        lick_times = sess_file['behavior_lick_times'] # lick_times[trial_id] is ndarray, each element is a list of lick times (need to subtract go cue time from it)\n   123→        correctness = sess_file['behavior_report'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64. 1: correct or free water, 0: error, -1: no response\n   124→        gocue_time = sess_file['task_cue_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is delay time, second row is duration of delay period\n   126→        sample_time = sess_file['task_sample_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is sample time, second row is duration of sample period\n   127→        delay_period = delay_time[1,:] # only need duration of delay period\n   128→        sample_period = sample_time[1,:] # only need duration of sample period\n   129→        stimulation = sess_file['task_stimulation'] # ndarray, (n_trials,4). The 4 columns are [laser_power, stim_type('1', '2', or '6', with 1,2,6 being left/right/both ALM perturbation), laser_on_time, laser_off_time] (with reference to trial start time - need to subtract go cue time from it).\n   130→        trial_type = sess_file['task_trial_type'] # ndarray, (1,n_trials), 'l' or 'r'\n   131→\n   132→        # align stimulation time and lick times to gocue_time=0 #\n   133→        lick_time_cal = []\n   134→        for i_trial, lick_times_trial in enumerate(lick_times):\n   135→            gocue_time_trial = gocue_time[0, i_trial]\n   136→            lick_time_cal.append(lick_times_trial - gocue_time_trial)\n   137→            stimulation[i_trial, 2:] -= gocue_time_trial\n   138→        lick_times = lick_time_cal\n   139→\n   140→        if i_file == 0:\n   141→            ### only save behavior and task info for the first probe file ###\n   142→            sess_dict = {}\n   143→            sess_dict['sess_name'] = sess_name\n   144→            sess_dict['auto_learn_trials'] = auto_learn_trials\n   145→            sess_dict['early_lick_trials'] = early_lick_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   146→            sess_dict['auto_water_trials'] = auto_water_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   147→            sess_dict['free_water_trials'] = free_water_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   148→            sess_dict['lick_directions'] = lick_directions # ndarray, (n_trials). Each element is a list of 0 (lick right) and 1 (lick left)\n   149→            sess_dict['lick_times'] = lick_time_cal # list with length n_trials, each element is 1D ndarray with all lick times in that trial\n   150→            sess_dict['gocue_time'] = gocue_time[0,:]\n   151→            sess_dict['correctness'] = correctness # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64. 1: correct or free water, 0: error, -1: no response\n   152→            sess_dict['delay_period'] = delay_period # (n_trials,) duration of delay period\n   153→            sess_dict['sample_period'] = sample_period # (n_trials,) duration of sample period\n   154→            sess_dict['stimulation'] = stimulation # ndarray, (n_trials,4). The 4 columns are [laser_power, stim_type('1', '2', or '6', with 1,2,6 being left/right/both ALM perturbation), laser_on_time, laser_off_time]. For no stim trials, we have [0, NaN, NaN, NaN]\n   155→            sess_dict['trial_type'] = 1*(trial_type=='l') # ndarray, (n_trials,). 1 is left, 0 is right 'r'\n   156→            # with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'behavior_task.pickle'), 'wb') as f:\n   157→            #     pickle.dump(sess_dict, f)\n   158→\n   159→            ### Read the neuron IDs after QC for this session ###\n   160→            '''\n   161→            The index refers to within individual SESSION, resulting in summing all units from all penetrations in a given Session. For instance, if in Session 08/17/22, we have 3 penetrations: p1 has 10 units, p2 has 30 units, p3 has 5 units (before filtering) - overall there are 45 units. An index of '42' from this session means the 2nd (42-10-30) unit from p3.\n   162→            '''\n   163→            temp_list = list(glob.glob(os.path.join(qc_folder, '{}*.mat'.format(sess_name_qc))))\n   164→            assert len(temp_list) == 1\n   165→            filepath_qc = temp_list[0]\n   166→\n   167→            qc_file = preprocessing_utils.loadmat(filepath_qc)\n   168→            # idx_qc_dict = qc_file['Idx'] # dict. Keys are 'region_qc' (e.g. 'ALM_qc'). Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n   169→            idx_qc_dict = {}# dict. Keys are 'region_qc' (e.g. 'ALM_qc'). Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n   170→            for area, id_list in qc_file['Idx'].items():\n   171→                if id_list == []:\n   172→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = []\n   173→                elif isinstance(id_list, int) or isinstance(id_list, float): # only one neuron\n   174→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = [id_list-1]\n   175→                else:\n   176→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = list(np.array(id_list) - 1)\n   177→\n   178→            anno_qc_dict = {}\n   179→            for name, value in qc_file['AnnoName'].items():\n   180→                if isinstance(value, str):\n   181→                    anno_qc_dict[name] = [value]\n   182→                else:\n   183→                    anno_qc_dict[name] = value\n   184→\n   185→        # --- 1.2 neural data ---\n   186→        # -- 1.2.1 spike times\n   187→        spike_times = sess_file['neuron_single_units'] # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   188→\n   189→        # -- 1.2.2 unit_info and QC stats\n   190→        unit_info = sess_file['neuron_unit_info'] # ndarray (n_units, 8)\n   191→        # neuron_unit_info: columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location. For putative type, we have: 'Pyr' - pyramidal neurons, 'FS' - fast spiking, 'not classified', 'all' - all types\n   192→        probe_no_col = probe * np.ones((unit_info.shape[0], 1)) # (n_units,1) original probe #\n   193→        unit_info = np.concatenate([unit_info, probe_no_col], axis=1) # (n_units, 9)\n   194→        unit_qc = sess_file['neuron_unit_quality_control']\n   195→        # dictionary. Needed keys: 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation', 'unit_snr', 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   196→\n   197→        # -- 1.2.3 histology\n   198→        histology = sess_file['histology'] # dictionary, keys: 'unit', 'ccf_x', 'ccf_y', 'ccf_z', 'annotation'\n   199→        ccf_unit_id = np.array(histology['unit']) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   200→        ccf_coordinate = np.stack([histology['ccf_x'], histology['ccf_y'], histology['ccf_z']]).swapaxes(0,1) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   201→        ccf_label = histology['annotation'] # list of np.str_\n   202→        ccf_label = [str(x).strip() for x in ccf_label] # list of string, with both whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   203→\n   204→        # -- 1.2.4 get units that have both ephys and histology\n   205→        unit_comb = set(unit_info[:,0]) & set(ccf_unit_id) # get the id of units that have both ephys and histology\n   206→        mask_ephys = [True if x in unit_comb else False for x in unit_info[:,0]] # mask for all other data (ephys) except histology\n   207→        mask_histology =[True if x in unit_comb else False for x in ccf_unit_id] # mask for histology only\n   208→\n   209→        # mask CCF info\n   210→        ccf_coordinate = ccf_coordinate[mask_histology,:] # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   211→        ccf_label = list(compress(ccf_label, mask_histology)) # list of string, with both whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   212→        ccf_unit_id = ccf_unit_id[mask_histology]\n   213→\n   214→        # mask ephys info\n   215→        unit_info = unit_info[mask_ephys,:] # (n_neurons, 9)\n   216→        spike_times = spike_times[mask_ephys]\n   217→        for name in unit_qc.keys():\n   218→            unit_qc[name] = list(np.array(unit_qc[name])[mask_ephys])\n   219→\n   220→        # --- 1.3 add probe data to sess data ---\n   221→        spike_times_sess.append(spike_times) # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   222→        unit_info_sess.append(unit_info) # (n_units, 9)\n   223→        unit_qc_sess_list.append(unit_qc) # dictionary. Needed keys: 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation', 'unit_snr', 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   224→        ccf_coordinate_sess.append(ccf_coordinate) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   225→        ccf_label_sess = ccf_label_sess + ccf_label # list\n   226→        ccf_unit_id_sess.append(ccf_unit_id) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   227→\n   228→    # ----- 2. combine data from all probes ---\n   229→    spike_times_sess = np.concatenate(spike_times_sess) # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   230→    unit_info_sess = np.concatenate(unit_info_sess, axis=0) # (n_units, 9)\n   231→\n   232→    # uncommented\n   233→    unit_qc_sess = {'unit_amp': [], 'presence_ratio': [], 'amplitude_cutoff': [], 'isi_violation': [], 'avg_firing_rate':[], 'drift_metric': []}\n   234→    for probe_qc_dict in unit_qc_sess_list:\n   235→        for qc_name in unit_qc_sess.keys():\n   236→            unit_qc_sess[qc_name] = unit_qc_sess[qc_name] + probe_qc_dict[qc_name]\n   237→\n   238→    ccf_coordinate_sess = np.concatenate(ccf_coordinate_sess, axis=0) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   239→    ccf_unit_id_sess = np.concatenate(ccf_unit_id_sess) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   240→\n   241→    n_neurons = len(spike_times_sess)\n   242→    assert unit_info_sess.shape[0] == n_neurons\n   243→    assert len(unit_qc_sess['drift_metric']) == n_neurons\n   244→    assert ccf_coordinate_sess.shape[0] == n_neurons\n   245→    assert len(ccf_label_sess) == n_neurons\n   246→    assert len(ccf_unit_id_sess) == n_neurons\n   247→    # print('total neurons:', n_neurons)\n   248→\n   249→    # ----- 3. Process neurons from areas of interest and perform QC filtering -----\n   250→    i_file = 1\n   251→    neuron_id_used = []\n   252→    neuron_id_all = np.arange(n_neurons) # neuron_id is continuous from 0. unit_id is the original id from DJ.\n   253→\n   254→    for region in ['ALM', 'Medulla', 'Midbrain', 'Striatum', 'Thalamus', 'Pons', 'Cerebellum', 'Hypothalamus', 'Hippocampus', 'Orbital', 'OtherCortex', 'Olfactory', 'CorticalSubplate', 'Pallidum']:\n   255→        for side in ['left', 'right']:\n   256→            area = '_'.join([side, region])\n   257→            area_key = '{}_qc'.format(region)\n   258→            neuron_id_area = helper_get_neuron_id_area(idx_qc_dict[area_key], anno_qc_dict[area_key], ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, side, region)\n   259→\n   260→            if len(neuron_id_area) == 0:\n   261→                continue\n   262→\n   263→            print('Filtered neurons:', area, len(neuron_id_area))\n   264→\n   265→            # record used neurons\n   266→            neuron_id_used = neuron_id_used + neuron_id_area\n   267→\n   268→            save_flag = process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n   269→\n   270→            if save_flag:\n   271→                i_file += 1\n   272→\n   273→    # ----- 4. Don't process the rest of the neurons -----\n   274→    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'others_finished.txt'), 'w') as f:\n   275→        f.write('{}: finished.'.format(sess_name))\n   276→\n   277→    '''\n   278→    neuron_id_others = set(neuron_id_all) - set(neuron_id_used)\n   279→    neuron_id_others = sorted(list(neuron_id_others))\n   280→\n   281→    if len(neuron_id_others)==0:\n   282→        return\n   283→\n   284→    print('Filtered neurons:', 'others', len(neuron_id_others))\n   285→    process_one_area(sess_name, 'others', neuron_id_others, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n   286→    '''\n   287→\n   288→\n   289→def process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict):\n   290→    \"\"\"\n   291→    Get all neurons in an area, extract firing rates, and then save data.\n   292→    \"\"\"\n   293→    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n   294→        return True\n   295→\n   296→    # filter using the neuron mask\n   297→    n_units = unit_info_sess.shape[0]\n   298→    spike_times_area = spike_times_sess[neuron_id_area]\n   299→    unit_info_area = unit_info_sess[neuron_id_area,:] # (n_units, 9)\n   300→\n   301→    # uncommented\n   302→    unit_qc_area = {}\n   303→    for qc_name in unit_qc_sess.keys():\n   304→        unit_qc_area[qc_name] = np.array(unit_qc_sess[qc_name])[neuron_id_area]\n   305→        assert len(unit_qc_sess[qc_name]) == n_units\n   306→\n   307→    ccf_coordinate_area = ccf_coordinate_sess[neuron_id_area,:] # ndarray (n_neurons,3)\n   308→    ccf_label_area = list(np.array(ccf_label_sess)[neuron_id_area])\n   309→    ccf_unit_id_area = list(ccf_unit_id_sess[neuron_id_area])\n   310→\n   311→    # truncate spike times into desired time window (already aligned to go cue in raw data)\n   312→    for i_cluster, st_cluster in enumerate(spike_times_area):\n   313→        for i_trial, st_trial in enumerate(st_cluster):\n   314→            # if spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial] != []:\n   315→            #     amin_all.append(np.amin(spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial]))\n   316→            #     amax_all.append(np.amax(spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial]))\n   317→            if isinstance(st_trial, float) or isinstance(st_trial, int):\n   318→                spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] = np.array([st_trial])\n   319→            # temp = spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial].copy()\n   320→            temp = spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] # in Susu's data, spike_times are relative to go cue time\n   321→            spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] = spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial][(temp>=begin_time)*(temp<=end_time)]\n   322→\n   323→    # bin spike times into firing rates\n   324→    bin_centers, fr = sliding_histogram(spike_times_area, begin_time, end_time, bin_width=bw, stride=stride, rate=True) # fr: (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n   325→\n   326→    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n   327→        with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area)), 'rb') as f: # HERE\n   328→            area_dict_exist = pickle.load(f)\n   329→            fr = area_dict_exist['fr']\n   330→            spike_times_area = area_dict_exist['spike_times']\n   331→            bin_centers = area_dict_exist['bin_centers']\n   332→\n   333→    # organize data into dict\n   334→    area_dict = {}\n   335→    area_dict['sess_name'] = sess_name # str\n   336→    area_dict['area'] = area # str\n   337→\n   338→    area_dict['bin_centers'] = bin_centers\n   339→    area_dict['fr'] = fr # (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n   340→    area_dict['spike_times'] = spike_times_area # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray with all spike times of this neuron in this trial. (go cue time is 0.)\n   341→    area_dict['neuron_info'] = unit_info_area # ndarray (n_units, 9)\n   342→    #  columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location, probe #. For putative type, we have: 'Pyr' - pyramidal neurons, 'FS' - fast spiking, 'not classified', 'all' - all types\n   343→    area_dict['unit_qc'] = unit_qc_area # dict. Keys are 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation',, 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   344→    area_dict['ccf_coordinate'] = ccf_coordinate_area # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   345→    area_dict['ccf_label'] = ccf_label_area # list of string, with whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   346→    area_dict['ccf_unit_id'] = ccf_unit_id_area # ndarray (n_neurons,), id of units with histology\n   347→\n   348→    for name, info in sess_dict.items():\n   349→        area_dict[name] = info\n   350→\n   351→    # save data\n   352→    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area)), 'wb') as f:\n   353→        pickle.dump(area_dict, f)\n   354→\n   355→    return True\n   356→\n   357→\n   358→\n   359→def helper_get_neuron_id_area(neuron_idx_list, anno_qc_list, ccf_coor, ccf_label, side, region):\n   360→    \"\"\"\n   361→    Filter the neurons in data_sess so that only neurons in side_region is left.\n   362→\n   363→    Args:\n   364→        neuron_idx_list (list): list of good neuron IDs.\n   365→        anno_qc_list (list): list of CCF annotations of good neurons.\n   366→        ccf_coor (ndarray): ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates.\n   367→        ccf_label (list): list of string (CCF annotations), with length n_neurons.\n   368→        side (str): 'left' or 'right'.\n   369→        region (str): 'ALM', 'Medulla', 'Midbrain', 'Striatum', 'Thalamus', 'BLA', or 'ECT'.\n   370→\n   371→    Returns:\n   372→        neuron_id_area (list): id of neurons within side_region.\n   373→    \"\"\"\n   374→    if len(neuron_idx_list) == 0:\n   375→        return []\n   376→\n   377→    n_neurons = ccf_coor.shape[0]\n   378→    assert len(ccf_label) == n_neurons\n   379→    neurons_all = np.arange(n_neurons)\n   380→\n   381→    # filter neurons using side\n   382→    ML_mid_coor = 5700\n   383→    if side == 'left':\n   384→        mask_side = ccf_coor[:,0] >= ML_mid_coor\n   385→    elif side == 'right':\n   386→        mask_side = ccf_coor[:,0] < ML_mid_coor\n   387→    neuron_id_side = neurons_all[mask_side]\n   388→\n   389→    if len(neuron_id_side) == 0:\n   390→        return []\n   391→\n   392→    # combine side filter with qc filter\n   393→    neuron_id_area = sorted(list(set(neuron_id_side) & set(neuron_idx_list)))\n   394→    neuron_id_area = [idx for idx in neuron_id_area if ccf_label[idx] != []]\n   395→    if len(neuron_id_area) == 0:\n   396→        return []\n   397→    ccf_label_list = list(np.array(ccf_label)[neuron_id_area])\n   398→    ccf_label_joined = ''.join(ccf_label_list)\n   399→\n   400→    idx_side = [i for i, idx in enumerate(neuron_idx_list) if idx in neuron_id_area]\n   401→    anno_qc_list = list(np.array(anno_qc_list)[idx_side])\n   402→    anno_qc_joined = ''.join(anno_qc_list)\n   403→\n   404→    # temp = list(np.array(ccf_label)[neuron_id_area])\n   405→    # print(len(temp), len(anno_qc_list))\n   406→    # num = np.amin([len(temp), len(anno_qc_list)])\n   407→    # for ii in range(num):\n   408→        # if temp[ii] != anno_qc_list[ii]:\n   409→        # print(ii, temp[ii], anno_qc_list[ii])\n   410→    assert ccf_label_joined == anno_qc_joined\n   411→\n   412→    '''\n   413→    # filter neurons using region\n   414→    if region == 'ALM':\n   415→        res = 100 # resolution: 100 um\n   416→        # ALM_voxels = np.load(open('ALM_voxels_raw.npy', 'rb')) # (n_voxels,3), xyz of ALM\n   417→        ALM_voxels = np.load(open('ALM_voxels_symmetric.npy', 'rb')) # (n_voxels,3), xyz of ALM regions\n   418→\n   419→        diff = ccf_coor[:,None,:] - ALM_voxels[None,:,:] # (n_neurons, n_voxels, 3)\n   420→        mask_region = (np.abs(diff[:,:,0]) <= res/2) * (diff[:,:,1] <= res/2) * (np.abs(diff[:,:,2]) <= res/2) # (n_neurons, n_voxels)\n   421→        mask_region = np.any(mask_region, axis=1) # (n_neurons,)\n   422→\n   423→        neuron_id_region = neurons_all[mask_region]\n   424→    else:\n   425→        mask_region, neuron_id_region = get_id_subarea(region, ccf_label)\n   426→    '''\n   427→\n   428→    # neuron_id_area = sorted(list( set(neuron_id_side) & set(neuron_id_region)))\n   429→    return neuron_id_area\n   430→\n   431→\n   432→def helper_filter_by_neuron_id(data_sess, neuron_id_area, probe_no, area_name):\n   433→    \"\"\"\n   434→    Using the given neuron_id_area, filter the neuron sin data_sess.\n   435→\n   436→    Arg:\n   437→        data_sess (dict): same format as sess_dict.\n   438→        neuron_id_area (list): list of neuron if to use.\n   439→        probe_no (int): probe number.\n   440→        area_name (str):  name of the area. e.g. 'left_ALM'.\n   441→\n   442→    Returns:\n   443→        data_area (dict): same format as sess_dict.\n   444→    \"\"\"\n   445→    data_area = {}\n   446→\n   447→    data_area['mouse'] = data_sess['mouse']\n   448→    data_area['date'] = data_sess['date']\n   449→    data_area['probe'] = probe_no\n   450→    data_area['area'] = area_name\n   451→\n   452→    data_area['bin_centers'] = data_sess['bin_centers']\n   453→    data_area['fr'] = data_sess['fr'][:,:,neuron_id_area] # Empty 0x1 cell. Need to manually define what it becomes in Python.\n2. 1x1 cell. Will be directly read as non-iterables such as str, int, float, etc. Need to manually convert it to an iterable with length 1.\n3. Nx1 cell (N >= 2). Usually it's fine to read and directly store it in Python.\n\nComment: in case 2, when using isinstance(data, data_type), if data could be a number, consider the possibility of data being either float or int, i.e. use\n    if isinstance(data, float) or isinstance(data, int):\n        do something\n\"\"\"\n\nplt.rcParams['pdf.fonttype'] = 42\nplt.rcParams['ps.fonttype'] = 42\n\n\ndef sliding_histogram(spikeTimes, begin_time, end_time, bin_width, stride, rate=True):\n    '''\n    Calculates the number of spikes for each unit in each sliding bin of width bin_width, strided by stride.\n    begin_time and end_time are treated as the lower- and upper- bounds for bin centers.\n    if rate=True, calculate firing rates instead of bin spikes.\n    '''\n    bin_begin_time = begin_time\n    bin_end_time = end_time\n    # This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    else:\n        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n\n    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n\n    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n\n    if rate:\n        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\n    return binCenters, binSpikes\n\n\ndef process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0, end_time=3.5, save_folder=os.path.join(os.pardir, 'data_processed'), qc_mode='classifier'):\n    \"\"\"\n    Process all probe files in one session.\n\n    Args:\n        data_folder (str): the folder containing all data including QC neuron ID files.\n        file_path_list: list of full file paths of all probes in this session.\n        bw (float): in sec.\n        stride (float): in sec.\n        qc_mode (str): 'classifier' or 'old'.\n    \"\"\"\n    print(qc_mode)\n    if qc_mode == 'classifier':\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'goodunits')\n        print(qc_folder)\n    elif qc_mode == 'old':\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'DJ_GoodUnitsIdx_14regions_old_qc')\n    else:\n        raise ValueError('Wrong qc_mode! First row is go cue time, second row is duration of response period\n        delay_time = sess_file['task_delay_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is delay time, second row is duration of delay period\n        sample_time = sess_file['task_sample_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is sample time, second row is duration of sample period\n        delay_period = delay_time[1,:] # only need duration of delay period\n        sample_period = sample_time[1,:] # only need duration of sample period\n        stimulation = sess_file['task_stimulation'] # ndarray, (n_trials,4). Keys are 'region_qc' (e.g. 'ALM_qc'). Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n            for area, id_list in qc_file['Idx'].items():\n                if id_list == []:\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = []\n                elif isinstance(id_list, int) or isinstance(id_list, float): # only one neuron\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = [id_list-1]\n                else:\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = list(np.array(id_list) - 1)\n\n            anno_qc_dict = {}\n            for name, value in qc_file['AnnoName'].items():\n                if isinstance(value, str):\n                    anno_qc_dict[name] = [value]\n                else:\n                    anno_qc_dict[name] = value\n\n        # --- 1.2 neural data ---\n        # -- 1.2.1 spike times\n        spike_times = sess_file['neuron_single_units'] # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n\n        # -- 1.2.2 unit_info and QC stats\n        unit_info = sess_file['neuron_unit_info'] # ndarray (n_units, 8)\n        # neuron_unit_info: columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location. Don't process the rest of the neurons -----\n    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'others_finished.txt'), 'w') as f:\n        f.write('{}: finished.'.format(sess_name))\n\n    '''\n    neuron_id_others = set(neuron_id_all) - set(neuron_id_used)\n    neuron_id_others = sorted(list(neuron_id_others))\n\n    if len(neuron_id_others)==0:\n        return\n\n    print('Filtered neurons:', 'others', len(neuron_id_others))\n    process_one_area(sess_name, 'others', neuron_id_others, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n    '''\n\n\ndef process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict):\n    \"\"\"\n    Get all neurons in an area, extract firing rates, and then save data.\n    \"\"\"\n    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n        return True\n\n    # Choice: left=0, right=1\n257:         instruction = trials['trial_instruction'][trial_idx]\n258:         outcome = trials['outcome'][trial_idx]\n259:         if outcome == 'hit':\n260:             choice = 0 if instruction == 'left' else 1\n261:         elif outcome == 'miss':\n262:             choice = 1 if instruction == 'left' else 0  # miss means licked wrong side\n263:         else:\n264:             choice = 0  # shouldn't happen after filtering\n265: \n266:         # 2. Outcome: ignore=0, miss=1, hit=2\n267:         outcome_map = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\n268:         outcome_val = outcome_map.get(outcome, 0)\n269: \n270:         # 3. Early lick: no=0, yes=1 (should be all 0 after filtering)\n271:         early_lick_val = 0 if trials['early_lick'][trial_idx] == 'no early' else 1\n272: \n273:         # 4. Tongue y-position (discretized per session) - get raw value first\n274:         # Get tongue y values during this trial window\n275:         abs_bin_starts = bin_centers + go_cue - BIN_WIDTH / 2\n276:         abs_bin_ends = bin_centers + go_cue + BIN_WIDTH / 2\n277:         tongue_y_bins = np.zeros(len(bin_centers), dtype=np.float32)\n278:         for b in range(len(bin_centers)):\n279:             mask = (tongue_times >= abs_bin_starts[b]) & (tongue_times < abs_bin_ends[b])\n280:             if np.any(mask):\n281:                 # Use likelihood-weighted mean, but only if likelihood > 0.5\n282:                 good_mask = mask & (tongue_likelihood > 0.5)\n283:                 if np.any(good_mask):\n284:                     tongue_y_bins[b] = np.mean(tongue_y[good_mask])\n285:                 else:\n286:                     tongue_y_bins[b] = np.nan\n287:             else:\n288:                 tongue_y_bins[b] = np.nan\n289: \n290:         tongue_y_trial_values.append(tongue_y_bins)\n291: \n292:         output_trial = np.array([choice, outcome_val, early_lick_val], dtype=np.int64)  # (3,) - tongue added later\n293: \n294:         neural_list.append(fr)\n295:         input_list.append(input_trial)\n296:         output_list.append(output_trial)\n297: \n298:     io.close()\n299: \n300:     return {\n... This gives human-readable subject identifiers consistent with the paper.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each NWB file represents one session. The session ID is taken from `nwb.session_id`. All data within one NWB file (trials, units, behavioral events) belong to that session.\n\nii.\n```python\nsession_id = nwb.session_id if nwb.session_id else os.path.basename(nwb_path)\n```\n\niii. The DANDI dataset organizes data as one NWB file per session, so this is a natural split.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are defined by the NWB trial table. The AI iterates over valid trial indices and processes each trial individually, extracting neural data, inputs, and outputs per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor trial_idx in valid_trial_indices:\n    go_cue = go_start_times[trial_idx]\n    obs_idx = trial_to_obs[trial_idx]\n    spike_times_trial = get_spike_times_for_trial_from_cache(all_spike_times, obs_intervals, obs_idx)\n    fr, bin_centers = compute_firing_rates(spike_times_trial, go_cue, BEGIN_TIME, END_TIME, BIN_WIDTH)\n```\n\niii. The AI handles the obs_intervals-to-trial mapping carefully, since some NWB files have fewer obs_intervals than trials (due to multi-session recordings within one file). Only trials with corresponding neural data are processed.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI excludes early lick trials (`early_lick != 'no early'`) and no-response/ignore trials (`outcome == 'ignore'`). Additionally, only trials with neural data coverage (present in obs_intervals) are included. The AI does NOT filter auto_water or free_water trials.\n\nii.\n```python\nif early != 'no early':\n    continue\nif outcome == 'ignore':\n    continue\n```\n\niii. The AI cited the methods text: \"Early lick trials and no response trials were excluded for analysis.\" The reference code's downstream analysis (`get_regular_trial_mask`) additionally filters auto_water, free_water, and stimulation trials, but the AI only applied the filters explicitly mentioned in the methods text. Stimulation trials are kept because the instructions require photostimulation as a decoder input.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from spike times of individual units in the NWB `units` table, specifically the `spike_times` column for units classified as `'good'`.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_spike_times = preload_spike_times(nwb, good_indices)\n# ...\nreturn [nwb.units['spike_times'][uid] for uid in unit_indices]\n```\n\niii. The AI preloads all spike times for good units into memory for efficiency, then extracts per-trial spike times based on obs_intervals.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into 50ms non-overlapping bins and divided by bin width to produce firing rates in Hz. The window is -2.5s to +1.5s relative to go cue onset, yielding 80 time bins per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nn_bins = int(round((end_time - begin_time) / bin_width))  # 80\nbin_edges = np.linspace(begin_time, end_time, n_bins + 1) + go_cue_time\ncounts, _ = np.histogram(st_window, bins=bin_edges)\nfr[i, :] = counts.astype(np.float32) / bin_width\n```\n\niii. The AI noted this is \"simpler than the sliding kernel approach in the original preprocessing code but appropriate for the decoder task.\" The reference code used sliding histogram with 40ms width and 3.4ms stride, but the instructions specify 50ms bins.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `classification == 'good'` in the NWB units table are included. Sessions with 0 good units are skipped.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor i in range(n_units):\n    if units['classification'][i] == 'good':\n        good_indices.append(i)\n\nif len(good_indices) == 0:\n    print(f\"  Skipping {os.path.basename(nwb_path)}: 0 good units\")\n    io.close()\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The AI identified that the `'good'` classification in NWB corresponds to the classifier-based QC described in the paper and reference code (`qc_mode='classifier'`). The paper describes region-specific logistic regression classifiers trained on manually curated labels.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to the go cue onset. Spike times are absolute in the NWB file; the AI subtracts the go cue time to create relative bin edges, then histograms spikes into those bins.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo_cue = go_start_times[trial_idx]\nbin_edges = np.linspace(begin_time, end_time, n_bins + 1) + go_cue_time\nbin_centers = (bin_edges[:-1] + bin_edges[1:]) / 2.0 - go_cue_time\n```\n\niii. The go cue times are extracted from `BehavioralEvents['go_start_times']`. The reference code's raw data had spike times already aligned to go cue, so this step is an adaptation to the NWB format.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The time bin size is 50ms (0.05s), producing 80 bins for the 4.0s window (-2.5 to +1.5s). No rebinning is applied; spikes are directly histogrammed into these bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_WIDTH = 0.05  # 50 ms bins\nBEGIN_TIME = -2.5\nEND_TIME = 1.5\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"50-ms-width bins for computing firing rates.\" The reference code used 40ms width with 3.4ms stride (sliding window), but the AI correctly follows the instructions for the decoder task.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `BehavioralEvents['sample_start_times']` timestamps and `BehavioralEvents['go_start_times']` timestamps, plus trial start times.\n\nii.\n```python\nbe = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents']\ngo_start_times = be.time_series['go_start_times'].timestamps[:]\nsample_start_times = be.time_series['sample_start_times'].timestamps[:]\n```\n\niii. The AI uses `sample_start_times` as the tone onset events, matching the paper's description of the auditory sample epoch.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds*?\n\ni. The AI finds the last `sample_start_time` that falls between trial start and go cue (to avoid replayed epochs from early licking). It then computes time from tone onset at each bin center. A fallback of `go_cue - 1.85` is used if no sample_start is found.\n\nii.\n```python\nvalid_samples = sample_start_times[\n    (sample_start_times >= t_start) & (sample_start_times < go_cue)\n]\nif len(valid_samples) > 0:\n    tone_onset = valid_samples[-1]\nelse:\n    tone_onset = go_cue - 1.85  # fallback\n\ntone_onset_rel = tone_onset - go_cue\ntime_from_tone = bin_centers - tone_onset_rel\n```\n\niii. The agent identified that early licking triggers tone replays, so there can be multiple sample_start_times per trial. Taking the last one before go cue gives the actual (non-replayed) tone onset. The typical offset is ~1.85s before go cue (0.65s sample + 1.2s delay).\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The tone onset time is converted to go-cue-relative coordinates, then subtracted from the bin centers (which are also go-cue-relative) to produce time-from-tone-onset at each bin. This is stored as a continuous time-varying signal with shape `(n_bins,)`.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_from_tone = bin_centers - tone_onset_rel  # time since tone onset at each bin\ninput_trial = np.stack([time_from_tone.astype(np.float32), photostim_on], axis=0)  # (2, n_bins)\n```\n\niii. Both neural bin centers and tone onset are in the same go-cue-relative coordinate system, ensuring alignment.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Photostimulation* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the trial table columns `photostim_onset`, `photostim_duration`, and trial `start_time`.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_onset_trial = trials['photostim_onset'][trial_idx]\n# ...\nonset_val = float(photostim_onset_trial)\ndur_val = float(trials['photostim_duration'][trial_idx])\n```\n\niii. The AI identified that `photostim_onset` is `'N/A'` for control trials and a numeric value (relative to trial start) for stimulation trials.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Photostimulation*?\n\ni. For each trial, if photostim_onset is not `'N/A'`, the onset time is converted from trial-start-relative to go-cue-relative coordinates. A binary vector is created where bins within the photostim window are set to 1.0, all others to 0.0.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_on = np.zeros(len(bin_centers), dtype=np.float32)\nif photostim_onset_trial != 'N/A':\n    onset_val = float(photostim_onset_trial)\n    dur_val = float(trials['photostim_duration'][trial_idx])\n    photostim_start_abs = t_start + onset_val\n    photostim_stop_abs = photostim_start_abs + dur_val\n    ps_start_rel = photostim_start_abs - go_cue\n    ps_stop_rel = photostim_stop_abs - go_cue\n    photostim_on[(bin_centers >= ps_start_rel) & (bin_centers < ps_stop_rel)] = 1.0\n```\n\niii. The AI verified that photostim onset is ~-1.2s relative to go cue with 0.5s duration, consistent with the paper's description of late-delay photoinhibition.\n\n## 4-c. How is the `input` *Photostimulation* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The photostim on/off times are converted to go-cue-relative coordinates and compared against the same bin centers used for neural data. The binary photostim vector has the same shape `(n_bins,)` as the neural data's time dimension.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_on[(bin_centers >= ps_start_rel) & (bin_centers < ps_stop_rel)] = 1.0\n```\n\niii. Same coordinate system (go-cue-relative) ensures alignment.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick direction choice* derived from?\n\ni. The AI reasons that on hit trials, the mouse licked the instructed side. On miss trials, the mouse licked the wrong side, so choice is opposite of instruction.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Lick direction choice*?\n\ni. Choice is inferred from the combination of instruction and outcome: hit = licked correct side (instruction direction), miss = licked wrong side (opposite of instruction). Left = 0, right = 1. The NWB data does not have an explicit \"lick direction\" column, so this inference is necessary.\n\nii.\n```python\nif outcome == 'hit':\n    choice = 0 if instruction == 'left' else 1\nelif outcome == 'miss':\n    choice = 1 if instruction == 'left' else 0\nelse:\n    choice = 0  # shouldn't happen after filtering\n```\n\niii. Since ignore trials are filtered out, only hit and miss remain. The mapping is: hit+left instruction -> left choice (0), hit+right instruction -> right choice (1), miss+left instruction -> right choice (1), miss+right instruction -> left choice (0).\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Directly from `trials['outcome']` which contains `'ignore'`, `'miss'`, or `'hit'`.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_map = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\noutcome_val = outcome_map.get(outcome, 0)\n```\n\niii. Direct mapping from string values to integers per the instructions.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Outcome*?\n\ni. Simple string-to-integer mapping: ignore=0, miss=1, hit=2. After filtering out ignore and early lick trials, only miss (1) and hit (2) actually appear.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_map = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\noutcome_val = outcome_map.get(outcome, 0)\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds*?\n\ni. The AI finds the last `sample_start_time` that falls between trial start and go cue (to avoid replayed epochs from early licking). It then computes time from tone onset at each bin center. A fallback of `go_cue - 1.85` is used if no sample_start is found.\n\nii.\n```python\nvalid_samples = sample_start_times[\n    (sample_start_times >= t_start) & (sample_start_times < go_cue)\n]\nif len(valid_samples) > 0:\n    tone_onset = valid_samples[-1]\nelse:\n    tone_onset = go_cue - 1.85  # fallback\n\ntone_onset_rel = tone_onset - go_cue\ntime_from_tone = bin_centers - tone_onset_rel\n```\n\niii. The agent identified that early licking triggers tone replays, so there can be multiple sample_start_times per trial. Taking the last one before go cue gives the actual (non-replayed) tone onset. The typical offset is ~1.85s before go cue (0.65s sample + 1.2s delay).\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The tone onset time is converted to go-cue-relative coordinates, then subtracted from the bin centers (which are also go-cue-relative) to produce time-from-tone-onset at each bin. This is stored as a continuous time-varying signal with shape `(n_bins,)`.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_from_tone = bin_centers - tone_onset_rel  # time since tone onset at each bin\ninput_trial = np.stack([time_from_tone.astype(np.float32), photostim_on], axis=0)  # (2, n_bins)\n```\n\niii. Both neural bin centers and tone onset are in the same go-cue-relative coordinate system, ensuring alignment.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Photostimulation* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the trial table columns `photostim_onset`, `photostim_duration`, and trial `start_time`.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_onset_trial = trials['photostim_onset'][trial_idx]\n# ...\nonset_val = float(photostim_onset_trial)\ndur_val = float(trials['photostim_duration'][trial_idx])\n```\n\niii. The AI identified that `photostim_onset` is `'N/A'` for control trials and a numeric value (relative to trial start) for stimulation trials.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Photostimulation*?\n\ni. For each trial, if photostim_onset is not `'N/A'`, the onset time is converted from trial-start-relative to go-cue-relative coordinates. A binary vector is created where bins within the photostim window are set to 1.0, all others to 0.0.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_on = np.zeros(len(bin_centers), dtype=np.float32)\nif photostim_onset_trial != 'N/A':\n    onset_val = float(photostim_onset_trial)\n    dur_val = float(trials['photostim_duration'][trial_idx])\n    photostim_start_abs = t_start + onset_val\n    photostim_stop_abs = photostim_start_abs + dur_val\n    ps_start_rel = photostim_start_abs - go_cue\n    ps_stop_rel = photostim_stop_abs - go_cue\n    photostim_on[(bin_centers >= ps_start_rel) & (bin_centers < ps_stop_rel)] = 1.0\n```\n\niii. The AI verified that photostim onset is ~-1.2s relative to go cue with 0.5s duration, consistent with the paper's description of late-delay photoinhibition.\n\n## 4-c. How is the `input` *Photostimulation* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The photostim on/off times are converted to go-cue-relative coordinates and compared against the same bin centers used for neural data. The binary photostim vector has the same shape `(n_bins,)` as the neural data's time dimension.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_on[(bin_centers >= ps_start_rel) & (bin_centers < ps_stop_rel)] = 1.0\n```\n\niii. Same coordinate system (go-cue-relative) ensures alignment.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick direction choice* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `trials['trial_instruction']` (left/right) and `trials['outcome']` (hit/miss/ignore).\n\nii.\n```python\ninstruction = trials['trial_instruction'][trial_idx]\noutcome = trials['outcome'][trial_idx]\nif outcome == 'hit':\n    choice = 0 if instruction == 'left' else 1\nelif outcome == 'miss':\n    choice = 1 if instruction == 'left' else 0\n```\n\niii. The AI reasons that on hit trials, the mouse licked the instructed side. On miss trials, the mouse licked the wrong side, so choice is opposite of instruction.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Lick direction choice*?\n\ni. Choice is inferred from the combination of instruction and outcome: hit = licked correct side (instruction direction), miss = licked wrong side (opposite of instruction). Left = 0, right = 1. The NWB data does not have an explicit \"lick direction\" column, so this inference is necessary.\n\nii.\n```python\nif outcome == 'hit':\n    choice = 0 if instruction == 'left' else 1\nelif outcome == 'miss':\n    choice = 1 if instruction == 'left' else 0\nelse:\n    choice = 0  # shouldn't happen after filtering\n```\n\niii. Since ignore trials are filtered out, only hit and miss remain. The mapping is: hit+left instruction -> left choice (0), hit+right instruction -> right choice (1), miss+left instruction -> right choice (1), miss+right instruction -> left choice (0).\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Directly from `trials['outcome']` which contains `'ignore'`, `'miss'`, or `'hit'`.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_map = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\noutcome_val = outcome_map.get(outcome, 0)\n```\n\niii. Direct mapping from string values to integers per the instructions.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Outcome*?\n\ni. Simple string-to-integer mapping: ignore=0, miss=1, hit=2. After filtering out ignore and early lick trials, only miss (1) and hit (2) actually appear.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_map = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\noutcome_val = outcome_map.get(outcome, 0)\n```\n\niii. The output_values list includes all three values (`['ignore', 'miss', 'hit']`) for completeness, even though ignore never appears after trial filtering.\n\n## 6-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. N/A - This dataset does not have a \"distance to reward zone\" output. The outcome variable is per-trial (constant across time bins), broadcast to shape `(n_bins,)` for the time-varying output format.\n\nii.\n```python\noutput_trial[1, :] = base_output[1]  # outcome (constant across time)\n```\n\niii. Outcome is a per-trial label, replicated across all time bins.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Early lick* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `trials['early_lick']` which contains `'no early'` or `'early'`.\n\nii.\n```python\nearly_lick_val = 0 if trials['early_lick'][trial_idx] == 'no early' else 1\n```\n\niii. Direct binary mapping from the trial table field.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Early lick*?\n\ni. Binary mapping: `'no early'` -> 0, anything else -> 1. Since early lick trials are filtered out, this is always 0, making it a trivially decodable variable (100% accuracy).\n\nii.\n```python\nearly_lick_val = 0 if trials['early_lick'][trial_idx] == 'no early' else 1\noutput_trial[2, :] = base_output[2]  # early_lick (constant across time)\n```\n\niii. The AI acknowledged this is trivial after filtering but kept it as required by the output specification.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Tongue y-position* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `BehavioralTimeSeries['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']` which contains (x, y, likelihood) per frame at 300 Hz from DeepLabCut tracking.\n\nii.\n```python\nbts = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries']\ntongue_ts = bts.time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\ntongue_data = tongue_ts.data[:]  # (n_frames, 3): x, y, likelihood\ntongue_times = tongue_ts.timestamps[:]\ntongue_y = tongue_data[:, 1]\ntongue_likelihood = tongue_data[:, 2]\n```\n\niii. Uses the side camera (Camera0) tongue tracking data, column index 1 for y-position.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Tongue y-position*?\n\ni. Tongue y values are extracted per trial within the time window, filtered by likelihood > 0.5, averaged within each 50ms bin, then discretized per session using 40th and 60th percentiles.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Filter by likelihood > 0.5\nlh_mask = tw_lh > 0.5\ntw_times_good = tw_times[lh_mask]\ntw_y_good = tw_y[lh_mask]\n\n# Bin into time bins using np.digitize\nabs_bin_edges = np.linspace(BEGIN_TIME, END_TIME, len(bin_centers) + 1) + go_cue\nbin_assignments = np.digitize(tw_times_good, abs_bin_edges) - 1\nfor b in range(len(bin_centers)):\n    b_mask = bin_assignments == b\n    if np.any(b_mask):\n        tongue_y_bins[b] = np.mean(tw_y_good[b_mask])\n```\n\niii. The AI uses a likelihood threshold of 0.5 to filter out low-confidence tracking points, then computes mean y-position per time bin.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Tongue y-position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Per-session percentiles (40th and 60th) are computed from all valid (non-NaN) tongue y values across all trials in the session. Values below p40 get class 0 (\"low\"), between p40 and p60 get class 1 (\"mid\"), above p60 get class 2 (\"high\"). NaN values (no valid tracking) are mapped to class 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nvalid_tongue = all_tongue_y[~np.isnan(all_tongue_y)]\np40 = np.percentile(valid_tongue, 40)\np60 = np.percentile(valid_tongue, 60)\n\n# In discretize_tongue_y:\nif np.isnan(val):\n    result[i] = 0  # no tongue visible = low position\nelif val < p40:\n    result[i] = 0\nelif val <= p60:\n    result[i] = 1\nelse:\n    result[i] = 2\n```\n\niii. The percentile computation matches the instructions. The NaN-to-0 mapping is a design choice: the AI reasoned that no tongue visible implies the tongue is retracted (low position).\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *Tongue y-position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Tongue tracking frames are binned into the same 50ms time bins used for neural data, using go-cue-relative coordinates. The tongue y output has shape `(n_bins,)`, matching the neural time dimension.\n\nii.\n```python\nabs_bin_edges = np.linspace(BEGIN_TIME, END_TIME, len(bin_centers) + 1) + go_cue\nbin_assignments = np.digitize(tw_times_good, abs_bin_edges) - 1\n```\n\niii. Same bin edges as neural data ensure temporal alignment.\n\n## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several missing data scenarios are handled:\n- **obs_intervals mismatch**: When NWB files have fewer obs_intervals than trials (multi-session recordings), the AI builds a mapping from obs_intervals to trials and only processes trials with neural data.\n- **Missing tongue tracking**: Bins without valid tongue tracking (likelihood < 0.5 or no frames) get NaN, which is discretized to class 0.\n- **Missing tone onset**: Falls back to `go_cue - 1.85` if no sample_start_times found.\n- **Missing brain region annotation**: Mapped to `'unknown'`.\n- **Empty spike times**: Neurons with no spikes get zero firing rates.\n\nii.\n```python\n# obs_intervals mapping\nif diffs[min_idx] < 0.5:\n    obs_to_trial.append(int(min_idx))\nelse:\n    obs_to_trial.append(-1)  # no match\n\n# Tongue tracking fallback\ntongue_y_bins = np.full(len(bin_centers), np.nan, dtype=np.float32)\n\n# Tone onset fallback\ntone_onset = go_cue - 1.85  # fallback: typical sample-delay duration\n\n# Brain region fallback\nif anno is None or anno == '' or anno == 'nan':\n    anno = 'unknown'\n```\n\niii. The AI documented these edge cases in CONVERSION_NOTES.md and verified them against the data.\n\n## 10-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is reading spike times from NWB files. The AI initially read spike times individually per unit per trial, which was extremely slow (>30 min for full dataset). After optimization with batch preloading, the bottleneck became the overall I/O of reading large NWB files.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Optimized: batch preload\ndef preload_spike_times(nwb, unit_indices):\n    return [nwb.units['spike_times'][uid] for uid in unit_indices]\n```\n\niii. The agent documented: \"Original code read spike times individually for each unit from NWB per trial (very slow, >30 min for full dataset). Fix: Added preload_spike_times() to batch-read all spike times upfront.\"\n\n## 10-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Several loops could be vectorized:\n- The `compute_firing_rates` function loops over neurons individually to histogram spikes. This could use 2D histogram or vectorized operations.\n- The `discretize_tongue_y` function loops over individual values.\n- The `build_obs_to_trial_map` function loops over obs_intervals to find matches.\n- The per-bin tongue y averaging loop (`for b in range(len(bin_centers))`) could be replaced with groupby-style operations.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Per-neuron loop in compute_firing_rates\nfor i, st in enumerate(spike_times_by_neuron):\n    counts, _ = np.histogram(st_window, bins=bin_edges)\n    fr[i, :] = counts.astype(np.float32) / bin_width\n\n# Per-value loop in discretize_tongue_y\nfor i, val in enumerate(tongue_y_values):\n    if np.isnan(val):\n        result[i] = 0\n    elif val < p40:\n        result[i] = 0\n```\n\niii. The neuron-by-neuron histogram is inherently hard to vectorize since each neuron has different numbers of spikes. The discretize_tongue_y function is easily vectorizable with np.digitize or np.where.\n\n## 10-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. The `map_region_to_major` function is called twice for each neuron: once when collecting all region labels (to build the `brain_regions` list) and again when creating the `brain_region_idx` arrays.\n\nii.\n```python\n# First call: collecting labels\nfor label in s['brain_region_labels']:\n    all_region_labels.add(map_region_to_major(label))\n\n# Second call: creating indices\nidx = np.array([brain_regions.index(map_region_to_major(label))\n                for label in s['brain_region_labels']], dtype=np.int64)\n```\n\niii. This duplication is minor in terms of performance since string matching is fast, but it could be avoided by caching the mapped region per neuron.\n\n## 10-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. Several outputs are computed but have limited utility after trial filtering:\n- **Early lick output**: Always 0 after filtering (trivially decodable at 100%).\n- **Outcome ignore class (0)**: Never appears after filtering, yet the output_values includes it.\n- **Brain region mapping to major regions**: The detailed-to-major region mapping is extensive (15+ categories with many string matching rules) but could be simplified since many regions have few neurons.\n- **Tongue y-position per-bin computation**: The detailed per-bin tongue y computation is expensive, and bins with NaN (no tongue visible, which is the majority of time bins since the tongue is only out during licking) all map to class 0.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Early lick - always 0 after filtering\nearly_lick_val = 0 if trials['early_lick'][trial_idx] == 'no early' else 1\n\n# Tongue y: many bins will be NaN -> class 0\ntongue_y_bins = np.full(len(bin_centers), np.nan, dtype=np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The early lick output is required by the instructions even though it provides no information after filtering. The detailed tongue y computation is necessary per the instructions but produces mostly class-0 values during non-licking periods.\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}
